On a GB10, `checkpoint` against `full` gave a mean KL of 0.0077 nats a token and the same top token at 98.4% of 768 positions; a cold prompt read 1.81x faster (8,192 tokens in 4.41 s against 7.96 s; 1.82x at 2,048), and serial decode took 52.3 against 50.1 ms a token. Each mode is exact within itself, and the [quantization guide](docs/quantization.md#nvidias-nvfp4-checkpoints-on-cuda) has the measurements and `tensorfold kl`, which compares two precisions on the same tokens. Lightning's NVFP4 checkpoint keeps `full` for now.
- Living Weights keeps a way back. Before a folder's first lesson, `--slide` keeps every file a lesson can change (its safetensors files, `model.safetensors.index.json` and `config.json`) in a hidden `.tensorfold-original/` folder inside it, with a `SHA256SUMS` manifest checked against the kept files: clones on APFS, Btrfs and XFS, and on other file systems copies after a free-space check, refused with HTTP 507 and the space needed when they do not fit. On an M3 Ultra, Nemotron 3.5 Lightning's 7 files (18.5 GB) took 5.3 s as clones and no disk; on a GB10's ext4, in a build for its own CPU, they took 35 s as copies and 18.5 GB (the Linux release computes SHA-256 about five times slower). Later lessons reuse the snapshot, and each server checks it against its manifest at its first lesson (2.6 s on the M3 Ultra), refusing to teach while it does not match. Model loaders read only a folder's own files, and `tensorfold info` leaves hidden folders out of a model's size.
- `tensorfold slide rollback FOLDER` undoes every lesson since the snapshot. It checks the snapshot against its manifest, makes every clone and checks the free space for any copies before it changes a file, puts each file back, checks every file's SHA-256, and only then removes what the lessons wrote: lesson files, writes cut short, and the fact graph files `--slide-graph` named, printing each path, including any outside the folder. Files a user added, files a lesson never writes and the snapshot stay. It took 5.6 to 5.7 s on the M3 Ultra (clones) and 58 to 91 s on the GB10 (copies, one file at a time), and on both the folder came back byte for byte and, served again, gave the untaught folder's replies token for token.
- `--slide` starts only with a key: `--api-key`, `--api-key-file` or `TENSORFOLD_API_KEY`. The key is read before the model loads, with the rules the server applies, so a key file that is missing, empty or readable by other users, or a key with whitespace in it, stops the start at once with a line naming the cause. The Living Weights guide and the README show a key file.
- Each server holds its model folder while it runs (shared, or exclusive with `--slide`): a server refuses a folder that a `--slide` server or a rollback holds, a rollback refuses a folder any server holds, and `--slide` refuses a folder that cannot be locked, where a plain server serves it and logs one line. The hold is advisory, so other tools that write the folder do not see it.
- Without `--slide` replies are unchanged: on an M3 Ultra and a GB10 this build gave 1.0.11's tokens in 17 exact cells, drafted and with `"draft": false`, and 12 multi-turn steps.
- `TENSORFOLD_REQUEST_LOG` creates its file owner-only (mode 0600, where it was 0644), so the prompts and tool content it records are not readable by other users on the machine. A log file that already exists keeps its mode; `chmod 600` it once.
- Recall, off unless asked for: `tensorfold serve MODEL --recall` keeps a record of the token ids the server has read and written, one record per tokenizer under `~/.cache/tensorfold/recall` (`--recall-dir` names another), and drafts from it. The copy window asks a stream's own context first and then the record; grafted lanes merge the record's distinct continuations with the context's, the context's first on ties. A copy goes in only when its expected tokens, at the landing rate earlier copies of its match length have shown, beat the stream's own rounds. A copy that misses still costs its round the drafts it took the place of, lowers its match length's rate and keeps that stream off the record for four rounds; once no rate pays, the copy window walks the record only for a probe, one ask in 32, backing off to one in 1,024 while probes miss. Grafted lanes, which no served engine turns on yet, walk the record at each ask while its grafts land and every eighth round otherwise, at most 64 candidates a walk. Every copied token is verified, so replies are the same tokens with Recall on, off or `--recall-read-only`. On an M3 Ultra Qwen3.8-27B wrote four requests repeated after a restart at 16 tokens a round against 3.2 to 6.5 with Recall off, 2.4x faster, and on a GB10 Nemotron 3.5 Lightning at 15 to 16 against 2.4 to 3.3, 3.0x faster; the same requests with one detail changed ran 0.99x to 1.02x, re-edits of an earlier file 1.01x to 1.14x, and fresh prose and chat beside a populated record within 0.3% of Recall off. With Recall off, replies and speed match the build without it on both chips. Each request adds what no earlier request read, from its prompt-cache resume point, and its reply when the reply ends; runs the record already holds are not added again, so a repeated request adds nothing. The record holds token ids only, in 0700 and 0600 files; each 4 MiB file of 1,048,576 ids takes 8 MiB of memory, plus a key table (8 MiB at the default cap), and past `--recall-gib` (1/16 GiB by default) the oldest file goes. `GET /v1/recall` reports it and `DELETE /v1/recall` empties it; [Recall](docs/recall.md) says what stays on disk and how to run benchmarks beside it. On CUDA a lone Nemotron stream's own driver reads the record too; Flash Next refuses `--recall` before it loads, since its replay engine copies on the GPU from its own history. A reply's `speculative.proposer` counters are now written from the request's own copy; they were parsed from a buffer the server freed first, which no reply showed until an engine sent counters.
- `GET /v1/models` tells a client which reasoning efforts each model takes, so a coding agent's effort picker can come from the server instead of a table kept by hand. An entry for a model whose chat template has a thinking switch, names effort levels or has a `</think>` token carries an OpenRouter-style `reasoning` object: `default_enabled` (false under `--no-thinking`), `default_effort` (only with `--reasoning-effort`) and `supported_efforts`, the levels the template names, highest first, then `none` when the template has a thinking switch. Qwen3.8-27B and Qwen3.8-Flash-Next list xhigh, medium, low and none; GLM-5.3-Flash lists max, high and low; Nemotron 3.5 Lightning and Qwen3.5-2B, whose templates name no level, give `default_enabled` alone. The server reads the template once at startup, and no reply changes (#819). Thanks to @kelchm.
- On CUDA the NVFP4 experts' prompt entries take the `skip` their decode entries already took and leave that expert's items alone, so a family that computes one expert itself, as GLM-5.3-Flash does with its BF16 shared expert, can plan an NVFP4 prompt's pairs together. `tf-cuda-test quant-ref` checks it for FP8 and NVFP4: the skipped expert's rows keep what the buffer held, and every other row equals the run without skip. Nemotron skips no expert, so its replies are the same tokens, and on a GB10 its NVFP4 cold prompts of 2k to 64k tokens ran within 0.3% of before (#764). Thanks to @Bizuayeu.
- Nemotron decodes faster on CUDA with our own kernels. A decode window's router (16 rows or fewer) stages its rows and 16 experts at a time with every load in flight and runs the old router's sums from shared memory, top-k loads every slice and bias before summing them, and add_moe_norm loads and adds a row on 1,024 threads. Every sum keeps its order, so the outputs are the old kernels' bytes: on a GB10 they matched the previous build and the host references at every row count from 1 to 4,096, and the glue test now runs the router at every decode window size and at an unaligned window, which keeps the old router. With Nemotron 3.5 Lightning one stream decoded about 0.9% faster drafted and 1.5% faster with `"draft": false`, on MLX 4-bit and NVFP4 alike, with the same rounds on the same window-cost table; shared rounds of 2 to 8 streams and cold prompts of 2k to 64k tokens ran within 0.6% of before or faster (#833). Thanks to @jschmied.
- GLM-5.3-Flash's KDA and router kernels ask the Metal compiler for the 1,024 threads a threadgroup they launch with. On an M1 Max the compiler had built both KDA pipelines for 512 threads and several router widths for fewer than 1,024, so those launches could leave outputs unwritten without an error. The kernels' arithmetic is unchanged, and `launch_check.zig` runs every router width and both KDA head counts at 1,024 threads against float64, with a 512-thread control and recurrent seams. On an M3 Ultra, whose compiler already allowed 1,024 threads, the checks pass, every GLM-5.3-Flash pipeline keeps its limit and its threadgroup memory (at most 24 KiB, under the 32 KiB of M1-family GPUs), and GLM-5.3-Flash with 8 of its 45 layers gave the same tokens as before, drafted and with `"draft": false`, resumed and concurrent, with decode and prompt speed within 0.25% (#708). Thanks to @mrv777.
- EXLR8's one-set projection kernel at 1, 2 and 4 rows alternates two input tiles, so each block needs one barrier instead of two; 8- and 16-row windows keep the old schedule. The sums and their order are unchanged: on an M3 Ultra every case of `tf-exlr8-check` gives the same bytes as before. There, over 12 layouts, the kernel takes 2% to 5% less GPU time at one row and up to 2% less at two, between 6% less and 2% more at four, and the same time at 8 and 16 rows, where the first version was 3% to 8% slower (#789). Thanks to @mrv777.
- `core/tile_qmm` adds prompt matmul tiles for Macs without tensor units, on MLX affine 4-, 6- and 8-bit codes in groups of 64: 32 x 32 output tiles with fp32 group sums in k order, tiles over rows sorted by expert with a fused gate-up SwiGLU, and a decode twin for 1 to 32 rows that gives the tiles' bits. No family uses them yet; the LFM2 family (#822) will. `tf-tile-check` checks the tiles, the twin and the grouped tiles bit for bit and against float64, and passes on the author's M4 Pro and on an M3 Ultra, with an error of at most 2.5e-3 of the terms' magnitude (#821). Thanks to @BlivionIaG.
- The MLX dequantizer in `core/quant` reads any code width from 2 to 8 bits. A 3-, 5-, 6- or 7-bit code can run into the next word, which it now reads, as the float64 reference already did, and a test checks 3- and 6-bit rows against the reference. Nothing served calls it yet, so no reply changes: the Mac and CUDA servers' code is the same with and without it, and Nemotron's tokens on a GB10 are unchanged, drafted, plain and concurrent (#810). Thanks to @BlivionIaG.
- `core/quant/experts.zig` stacks a MoE layer's routed experts with the shared expert last (gate and up fused per expert), builds its router rows with the shared gate last, and slices both for one rank with the map from global to local expert ids, for any checkpoint table. The Qwen3.5 loader of the opt-in HIP build (#626) reads its experts through it, and Flash Next's HIP loader will too. A test stacks two experts and a shared one and checks their order and a rank's ids. The Mac and CUDA servers do not call it, and their code is unchanged (#811). Thanks to @BlivionIaG.
- `core` reads NVIDIA's ModelOpt NVFP4 checkpoints as stored. `modelopt.admit` checks config.json's quantization block (top level or `text_config`'s) for NVFP4 weights in groups of 16 and refuses other ModelOpt formats by name, and `nvfp4.zig` maps a linear's e2m1 codes, e4m3 scales and fp32 scale and gives a float32 reference row. Which linears are NVFP4 comes from the tensors, since nvidia/GLM-5.3-Flash-NVFP4 at 423acf37 stores its MTP layer in BF16 without listing it. The tests read that revision's config.json and its whole tensor header, 147,661 names. The loader that calls it comes with GLM-5.3-Flash on CUDA (#758). Thanks to @Bizuayeu.
- `Checkpoint.release` drops a checkpoint's pages from the page cache on Linux (madvise and fadvise DONTNEED) once a loader is done with them: on unified memory the page cache holds memory the device needs. The mapping stays valid, and a later read faults the same bytes back in. On a GB10 it dropped all 20.1 GiB that a read of Nemotron 3.5 Lightning's NVFP4 checkpoint had cached, in 0.26 s. Nemotron's own loader reads with direct I/O and leaves nothing behind, so its loads are unchanged; the saving is for loaders that read through the mapping, as GLM-5.3-Flash's will. Ours: a file whose mapping fell back to a heap copy (Zig's fallback when mmap fails) keeps its bytes, since advising that copy away would have zeroed them (#766). Thanks to @Bizuayeu.
- `Nvfp4Experts.packGateUp` packs gate and up straight into their stacked places, with no scratch copies. A loader packs every routed expert this way, and the copies cost about 20 s of a GLM-5.3-Flash load at TP=2. The words are the same, which a host test checks against `packOne`, and `tf-cuda-test quant-ref` gives the same numbers as before (#763). Thanks to @Bizuayeu.
- `cuda/rank_scatter.zig` sums tensor-parallel ranks' fp32 partial rows in rank order over NCCL send and receive: each rank receives only its own rows and ends with the bits an all-gather and a rank-order sum give, where NCCL's own reduce-scatter adds in its ring's order. `tf-cuda-test rank-scatter` checks it on real ranks, and `tf-cuda-test rank-sum` checks the sum kernel alone on one GPU for 2 to 5 ranks. On pairs of GB10s over RoCE, run by @Bizuayeu, @cwschroeder and @SvangenStudios, every rank's rows matched that reference byte for byte at 1 to 2,048 rows of 4,096 with three seeds each, and 2,048 rows took 1.0 to 1.06 ms with four NCCL channels; ours ran on one GB10. GLM-5.3-Flash at TP over hosts is its first caller (#642). Thanks to @Bizuayeu, and to @cwschroeder and @SvangenStudios for their runs.
- The native CUDA host takes a family's prompt cache, its rules and its budget from the family's own declarations (`snap_*`, `snap_grid`, `warm_quiet`), and a family split over hosts can declare the weights its rank holds and its own text for an open error. Nemotron declares what the host used to wire in by name, so its cache, warm turns and `--slide` are unchanged: on a GB10 its multi-turn chats resumed with the same cached tokens, step for step, and the same replies as before and as a server with the cache off, on MLX 4-bit and NVFP4 (#762). Thanks to @Bizuayeu.
- On CUDA the NVFP4 lane matmul and prompt GEMM can write the fp32 sums they hold instead of rounding them to bf16 (`qlinear`'s `decodeF32` and `promptF32`), so a tensor-parallel family can add its ranks' partial rows before the one rounding; other formats refuse fp32 out by name. The bf16 kernels are untouched: on a GB10 each of them compiles to the same SASS as before, `tf-cuda-test quant-ref` checks the fp32 outputs against float64, at every row count and as the unrounded bf16 outputs, and Nemotron NVFP4 gave the same tokens as before drafted, resumed and concurrent, with one stream's decode within 0.8% of before, 2 to 8 streams within 1.7% and cold prompts of 2k to 64k tokens within 0.4%. GLM-5.3-Flash on CUDA is its first caller (#757). Thanks to @Bizuayeu.
- Flash Next prefills each chat reply for the next turn in the background, as the 27B on a Mac and Nemotron on CUDA do, so a follow-up that sends the reply back (its content, the reasoning dropped) resumes past it instead of at the turn's start; a request that arrives meanwhile goes first. On an M3 Ultra with the 6-bit checkpoint and conversations of 7,000 to 8,000 tokens, follow-ups sent a second after each reply prefilled 19 to 26 tokens instead of 223 to 284 and started in 0.17 to 0.19 s instead of 0.41 to 0.46 s. Replies were the same tokens with and without it (drafted, `"draft": false` and every follow-up), multi-turn chats equalled a cache-off server's, and decode and cold prompts of 8k to 32k tokens stayed within 0.4%.
- `--learn-conversations` keeps every prompt state a conversation leaves in RAM on disk too, written while the engine idles and finished on a planned stop, so its next turn after a restart, a crash or an eviction reads the state back where that is quicker than prefilling. Rows two states share are stored once (ten conversations with one long system prompt store it once), every piece read is checked against its hash, and a read-back state gives the same reply as a fresh prompt pass. Qwen3.8-27B, Nemotron, Flash Next and GLM-5.3-Flash on one Mac and Nemotron on CUDA keep them; `--learn-daily-gib` caps the writes a day, and `POST /v1/imprint/resume`, `POST /v1/imprint/load` and `DELETE /v1/imprint` report, load and forget a conversation's states. It is off unless asked for. Without the flag no state is written, replies and speed are as before, and the three routes answer 404 naming the flag. On a GB10 with Nemotron 3.5 Lightning 4-bit, a 39,228-token conversation's next turn, 6.2 s cold, started in 0.25 s after a restart (314 MiB read in 0.15 s), in 1.75 s after the server was killed (8,500 tokens past the last state written were prefilled again) and in 0.19 s after five other conversations had pushed it out of RAM, each time with the cold reply's tokens and logprobs. On an M3 Ultra the 27B's 21,306-token next turn, 103 s cold, started in 0.83 s after a restart (1.5 GiB read in 0.41 s), in 33 s after a kill and in 0.72 s after an eviction, again with the cold reply's tokens.
- Flash Next on a Mac keeps its memory wired between requests: its buffers are held in a residency set, and the idle keepalive's tick, now a small copy on the GPU, keeps the set resident for `--keep-warm` seconds after each request (15 minutes by default), so the first token after a few seconds idle no longer waits for macOS to wire the model again. On an M3 Ultra with the 6-bit checkpoint (171.7 GiB held), a cached prompt's first token after 2, 5 and 10 s idle came in 0.13 to 0.15 s instead of 1.95 to 2.61 s, and a new 2,000-token prompt's in 1.96 to 2.09 s instead of 3.71 to 4.25 s; cold 8k-token prompts ran 4% faster, decode stayed within 0.3% and every reply was the same tokens. `FLASHNEXT_NO_RESIDENCY=1` leaves the set out (#732). The 27B's set now holds its kept prompt states too: on an M3 Ultra a cached prompt's first token after 5 and 10 s idle came in 0.17 to 0.19 s instead of 0.33 to 0.35 s, and on an M5 Ultra 0.1 to 0.2 s sooner up to 8 s idle and within about 0.1 s either way after that. Every residency set, Flash Next's, the 27B's, GLM-5.3-Flash's opt-in `GLM_RESIDENCY` set and the cluster loader's, now stays inside 70% of RAM with a 2 GiB margin, or is refused with a log line that says why, a RAM reading that failed included. Buffers made after a set is built join it inside the same limit (kept prompt states at the next idle tick, a new 27B stream's slot at once), and a kept state leaves its set before it is freed, since a set keeps a buffer's memory until a commit removes it. Thanks to @sethforprivacy, to @BobClawblaw, whose measurements on #732 showed that set changes belong between requests, and to @cwschroeder for the first M3 Ultra receipt.
- An Intel XPU backend, built only when asked for (`zig build xpu-native xpu-qwen27`, with Intel's `ocloc` and oneDNN's nGEN headers): Qwen3.8-27B on an Intel Arc Pro B70 (Xe2) over Level Zero, served by `tensorfold-native` through the lane host with DFlash2 chains and trees, shared rounds for concurrent streams, the prompt cache and structured output, from MLX affine 4/64 or symmetric GPTQ 4/128 checkpoints. The kernels are OpenCL C built by `ocloc` for the card, and the int4 prompt GEMMs are generated by nGEN at build time. A stream that does not fit the card's free memory is refused when the server starts, and the KV cache stays bf16 unless `XPU_KV_INT8` asks for int8. On the author's B70 (capped at 230 W) drafted replies equal `"draft": false`, a resumed prompt equals a fresh one and 1 to 8 concurrent streams equal their solo runs, with the bf16 cache and the int8 one; from the GPTQ 4/128 checkpoint, cold prompts of 2k to 64k tokens ran at 2,051 to 1,473 tok/s against llama.cpp SYCL's 788 to 600 (Q4_0) on the same card, and decode at 33.5 tok/s without a drafter (llama.cpp 27.7) and with DFlash2 at 122.9 on code and 99.3 on chat. We have no Intel GPU, so it lands on those receipts and our host checks: the XPU code compiles against this release's engine on Linux, the Metal and CUDA servers built with it have the same code and data bytes as without it, and a scan of its OpenCL and nGEN sources against the open kernels we keep found no copied code (#802). Thanks to @Mirrdhyn.
- The Linux release archive's CUDA kernels carry Ampere sm_80 (the A100) beside sm_86, sm_120 and sm_121, and `dist` refuses a fatbin that lacks any of the four. A ModelOpt checkpoint (FP8 or NVFP4) on a card below compute capability 9.0 is refused with the card, its sm and the reason: those formats meet their decode sums in a thread-block cluster. The floor itself is where it was, and the 4-bit checkpoints run on those cards. On the author's GA100 (sm_80) all 34 kernels build for compute_80 and 16 kernel checks pass, `glue` byte-equal to the host references; on a GB10 we built the release set for sm_80, 86, 120 and 121 and `check-fatbins` passes it, Nemotron 3.5 Lightning NVFP4 served there gives the same tokens as before, and the kernel contract now checks every registered launch's registers and shared memory on sm_80 too (#846). Thanks to @liumorrisclaw.
- The kernel contract covers this release's new GPU kernels. On Metal, `core/tile_qmm`'s prompt tiles and their decode twin at 4, 6 and 8 bits (the twin bit-equal to the tiles at all 12 shapes of each width), served at the expert shapes of LFM2.5-24B-A2B, which #822 will run on them; on CUDA, prompt16's and qmmf's FP4 tiles that write fp32 (#757), with the K slices met in a cluster, fused, or summed by `reduce_kernel<true>`, and the reduce-scatter's `tf_rank_sum` (#642). Each passes gates 1 to 4 against float64, tile_qmm on an M3 Ultra and an M5 Ultra and the CUDA kernels on a GB10, with every planted control red. Gate 5 holds their launches against every chip: tile_qmm's 20 pipelines on every Apple GPU family (on M1 and M2 by offline compiles, or by the tiles' own thread request), and the ten new CUDA launches on sm_80 to sm_121.
- `core/tile_qmm`'s tile kernels ask the Metal compiler for the 128 threads a threadgroup they launch with. Without the request no offline compile could show the thread count an M1 or M2 would give them, and those are the chips the tiles are for; with it the compiler must give 128. Their arithmetic is unchanged: the twin still equals them bit for bit, and `tf-tile-check` passes on an M3 Ultra and an M5 Ultra with the same float64 errors as before, to the last digit.
- GLM-5.3-Flash's `hc_expand_10` asks the Metal compiler for the 1,024 threads a threadgroup it launches with at every decode step. Compiled offline for M1 and M2, the kernel built for 928 threads without the request, the class of fault #708 fixed for KDA and the router; with it, 1,024. Its arithmetic is unchanged, `launch_check.zig` runs it at 1, 5 and 16 rows against float64, and the kernel contract records its launch.
- The opt-in HIP build has the model-free kernels a model engine composes, on #463's runtime: elementwise ops and casts, norms and the merged decode tails, RoPE, attention (the flat decode walk, a walk over a page table that reads a KV head once for the query heads that share it, and the 64-row prompt tile, flat and paged), the DeltaNet recurrence in fp32 at every length, the linear attention's conv and gates, the MoE router, pick and route, argmax and top-k, and a lane round's plan kernels. `zig build hip-kernel-test` checks each against its host reference, and a 200-row prompt cut at 1, 63, 64, 65 or 136 rows gives one call's rows and state bit for bit. In the author's runs at this change it passed 43 of 43 on a Radeon PRO W7800 (gfx1100) and a Radeon PRO V620 (gfx1030); gfx1151 and gfx1201 have not run this version. We have no AMD card, so on our side the HIP host tests pass on macOS and Linux and the GPU tests compile against stub kernels. Nothing serves on these kernels in this change, and nothing HIP is in the release archives (#617). Thanks to @BlivionIaG, and to @ThinkOffApp for the gfx1151 run of an earlier version.
- The opt-in HIP build runs MLX's affine 2- to 8-bit products: decode tiles (row, column, split, RDNA2's wide tile, and a stream tile for up to four products that share an input or a stacked gate and up with its activation), prompt tiles (the dot2 GEMM tile, K-parallel tiles for short prompts, and gfx11's matrix-core GEMM tile through the compiler's WMMA builtins, so no rocWMMA header is needed), plans over stacked experts and the embedding gather. The kernel registry (#613) picks each product's tile from tuning tables for gfx1030 and gfx1100, and RDNA4 takes the dot2 tiles. A row writes the same bytes alone and in a round of 2 to 32 rows, and the tiles the registry picks between by row count write equal bytes, which `hip-kernel-test` checks with every tile against float64. The author's runs of these sources passed `hip-kernel-test` 57 of 57, `hip-gpu-test` 30 of 30 and `hip-affine-test` 27 of 27 on gfx1100 and gfx1030, and @ThinkOffApp's the same on gfx1151 with HIP 7.14 and with Ubuntu's packaged HIP 7.1, and @jkuepker's run on gfx1201 (an RDNA4 Radeon AI PRO R9700, whose dot2 tiles none of those cards take) passed `hip-affine-test` 30 of 30, `hip-kernel-test` 61 of 61 and `hip-gpu-test` 36 of 36 (#621). Thanks to @BlivionIaG, @ThinkOffApp and @jkuepker.
- The HIP engine has a checkpoint loader for Qwen3.5 and Qwen3.6 text checkpoints, on the host: the geometry from `config.json` (dense and MoE, MTP layers, rotary, layer kinds), MLX's affine tables for 2- to 8-bit, one tensor index over the shards with the routed experts stacked, and the tensor-parallel cuts. It refuses what the HIP kernels do not compute (an activation other than SiLU, a sigmoid attention gate, an attention bias, a recurrent state other than fp32, sizes past their 32-bit counts), and gives an MTP head that a conversion shipped zero-centered its `1 + w` back. `zig build test-qwen35` runs its tests, and with `TF_QWEN_DIR` naming a checkpoint it reads every tensor in it: on a GB10 it read five MLX 4-bit checkpoints, Qwen3.5 0.8B to 9B and Qwen3.6-35B-A3B, with every tensor resolved (#626). Thanks to @BlivionIaG.
- Qwen3.5 and Qwen3.6 serve on one AMD GPU through the opt-in HIP build, on #626's loader and the kernels of #617 and #621: `zig build native-hip -Dhip-arch=gfx1100` builds a server with lane rounds and MTP drafts, the paged attention cache, the radix prompt cache, prompts in chunks (512 rows while other streams decode), graph-replayed rounds and a memory plan, and `tf-qwen35-test check` runs the model checks (drafted equals serial, solo equals together, graph equals eager, resumed equals fresh). It refuses `--speed-up`, and `matrix=on` on RDNA4 and RDNA2, which keep the dot2 tiles; more than one GPU comes with #655, below. The loader drops each part's mapped pages once the part is on the GPU. At this version the author's model checks passed on a Radeon PRO W7800 (gfx1100) and a Radeon PRO V620 (gfx1030), 0 failed on each: Qwen3.5-9B 42 passed, Qwen3.6-35B-A3B with its MTP head 46, Qwen3.8-27B 42, all MLX 4-bit (the 9B and the 27B carry no draft head, so their four drafted checks skip). Earlier versions served the 35B-A3B on the W7800 at 176.5 tok/s for code and 140.8 for chat, every reply equal to its solo and `"draft": false` runs, and @ThinkOffApp's gfx1151 runs matched drafted and serial output on three public checkpoints. Not run at this version: gfx1151 and gfx1201 (#631). Thanks to @BlivionIaG and @ThinkOffApp.
- The HIP Qwen3.5 engine serves structured output (`response_format` and the `guided_*` fields). The lane core's grammar makes every mask: each verify row is drawn among its allowed tokens, the first token is drawn again under the mask from the prompt's last row, and the MTP drafts reach the grammar as host tokens, so structured streams keep drafting. Unmasked rows take the same draws as before. At this version the author served Qwen3.5-9B on a Radeon PRO W7800 (gfx1100) and Qwen3.6-35B-A3B on a Radeon PRO V620 (gfx1030), both MLX 4-bit with an MTP head, four streams a server: JSON object, JSON schema, `guided_json`, regex, choice, grammar, `structured_outputs` and plain requests at temperature 0 and 0.8, thinking off and on, gave 32 of 32 valid replies on each card, each drafted reply equal to its `"draft": false` one; the 32 sent four at a time equalled their solo runs, and five malformed requests were refused with 400. Not run: RDNA3.5 and RDNA4 (#697). Thanks to @BlivionIaG.
- The HIP Qwen3.5 engine splits a model over 2, 4 or 8 GPUs (`--tp`, `--rank`, `--master`, `--master-port`) through RCCL, opened at run time from the system's ROCm behind `hip/collective.zig`. Rank 0 serves HTTP and sends the other ranks its policy and its steps over a TCP link. Before RCCL starts, every rank must match rank 0's world size, GPU, RCCL version, executable and checkpoint (config.json, the weight index, and each shard's header and sampled tensor bytes; paths may differ), or every rank refuses, naming the rank and what differs. A refusal during startup on any rank reaches every rank over the link, and a rank that cannot record a round's graphs stops every rank rather than leave the others waiting in a collective. Every rank sizes its streams for rank 0's window, and partial sums add in a fixed order (one add at two ranks, rank order after an all-gather at four and eight). Rounds replay graphs cut at each collective at every world size; `graphs=off` turns them off. At this version the author's model checks with Qwen3.6-35B-A3B (MLX 4-bit, with its MTP head) passed 19, 0 failed, at tp 2 on two Radeon PRO W7800s (gfx1100), at tp 2 on two V620s (gfx1030) and at tp 4 on four V620s; they compare each group's rounds with one rank's. In earlier versions four streams of code ran at 372.6 tok/s on two W7800s against 283.7 on one, and replayed graphs ran at least as fast as eager rounds under tp in every case the author measured but one: Qwen3.5-9B at tp 2 on the W7800s, 5.5% slower at one stream and 8% at four. Not run: tp 8, RDNA3.5 and RDNA4 groups, and a GPU group with the agreement checks added at landing (host tests run them over loopback TCP) (#655). Thanks to @BlivionIaG.
- The opt-in HIP build moves the pieces every HIP family shares out of the Qwen3.5 engine: the memory budget (`hip.admission.budget`, with `TENSORFOLD_HIP_MEMORY_LIMIT_GB`), the routed MoE and the ranks' fp32 reduce (`hip.moe`, `hip.reduce`), and the token draw, greedy, sampled and grammar-masked (`zig/src/hip_serve`). A routed view can plan its rows' own pairs instead of a power-of-two bucket (`exact`: 12 items for one decode row where the bucket takes 198), for a family whose launches no graph replays; Qwen3.5 keeps its buckets. In the author's runs Qwen3.5 decode logits stayed byte-identical to #655's on gfx1100 and gfx1030 with the 9B, the 35B-A3B and the 27B (#812). Thanks to @BlivionIaG.
- On HIP the MoE route lays out each expert's members and tiles with a block scan instead of one thread, and the decode pick runs 512 threads a block, one expert a thread at 512 experts. The starts, ends, picks and weights are the same, and the route test adds 513 experts at one decode row's 11 pairs and at a prompt's 2,816. In the author's runs `hip-kernel-test`, `hip-gpu-test` and `hip-affine-test` passed 134 of 134 on gfx1100 and gfx1030; on four V620s the route took 6.9 us instead of 30.8 and the pick 14.7 instead of 25.5, and Qwen3.5 decode logits stayed byte-identical (#813). Thanks to @BlivionIaG.
- The opt-in HIP build has Flash Next's kernels: the residual streams and their tensor-parallel sums, the n-gram rows, the indexer and the sparse attention, each checked against a host reference. The gated norm takes its gate as SiLU (Qwen3.5, the same bytes as before) or sigmoid (Flash Next's DeltaNet output norm), and the chain test fails when either gated-norm kernel ignores it. In the author's runs `hip-kernel-test`, `hip-gpu-test` and `hip-affine-test` passed 74 of 74 on gfx1100 and gfx1030. Flash Next serves on them with #816, below (#814). Thanks to @BlivionIaG.
- The opt-in HIP build admits Flash Next (`qwen4_exp`): a rank's weights as tensor parallelism splits them, a position's keys and a stream's state, checked against the published 3-, 4- and 6-bit checkpoints, a window's n-gram rows hashed on the host as the reference model does, and a refusal that names the card's grant and the scratch it keeps when a window does not fit. Flash Next's affine spec moves to its own file and its config and index readers no longer import core's files, so a backend that imports core as a module can read them; Flash Next on Metal now looks a matrix's width up through `widths.quantization`, and on an M3 Ultra its 6-bit checkpoint gave the same tokens as before, drafted and with `"draft": false` (#815). Thanks to @BlivionIaG.
- Flash Next serves on AMD GPUs through the opt-in HIP build: a device's forward (weights, caches, the n-gram tables in host memory, every layer and the MTP head) and the lane backend, with MTP drafts, a round's windows in one forward and prompt reuse at 64-row cuts; `zig build tf-flashnext-hip-test` runs its model checks on the GPU. The DeltaNet output norm takes the gate `config.json` declares (sigmoid, or SiLU), and a kept prompt whose restore fails part way is prefilled from the start on fresh caches. No public checkpoint fits on one 48 GB card, so its receipts are the tensor-parallel runs below (#816). Thanks to @BlivionIaG.
- Flash Next on HIP splits over 2, 4 or 8 GPUs: each rank holds its cut of the weights, the block outputs' shares are summed in the stream norm and the logits joined, followers run rank 0's steps, and every rank sizes its streams for rank 0's window and agrees on the streams the group's memory holds. Every rank must match rank 0's world size, GPU, RCCL version, executable and checkpoint before RCCL starts (#655's join), startup takes its steps over the link so a refusal on any rank reaches every rank, and a kept prompt's restore holds on every rank or none: all resume, or all start the prompt over. At this version the author's greedy runs of `tf-flashnext-hip-test generate` answered at tp 2 on two Radeon PRO W7800s (gfx1100; Qwen3.8-Flash-Next MLX 3-bit, 32.08 GiB a card) and at tp 4 on four Radeon PRO V620s (gfx1030; 4-bit, 21.58 GiB a card), each with a hash of every step's logits. Not run: tp 8, RDNA3.5 and RDNA4 groups, and a GPU group with the agreement checks added at landing (host tests run them over loopback TCP) (#817). Thanks to @BlivionIaG.
- Flash Next's HIP rounds replay graphs split at the collectives, by shape, with host inputs staged in one upload and streams in kept cache slots; `graphs=off` keeps rounds eager. A rank that cannot record a round's graphs stops every rank rather than leave the others waiting in a collective, and a slot keeps its caches when a reset fails, so a replayed graph never names freed buffers. At this version, in the author's runs at tp 2 on two Radeon PRO W7800s (gfx1100, 3-bit) and at tp 4 on four Radeon PRO V620s (gfx1030, 4-bit), graphs on and graphs off gave the same logits at every step, twice each, equal to #817's runs. In runs of an earlier version with the same cards and checkpoints, `serve_check` passed 15 of 15, the rewind gate gave 0 differing replies against a server with the prompt cache off, and every reply equalled its solo and `"draft": false` runs; one stream decoded code at 96.7 tok/s on the V620s and 105.7 on the W7800s, and four streams 164.2 and 149.6 in total. Not run at this version: those served checks, and tp 8, RDNA3.5 and RDNA4 (#818). Thanks to @BlivionIaG.
- The native tokenizer reads an ASCII bracket class inside `(?i:...)`, folded to its case variants as Oniguruma folds a literal there: LFM2's pre-tokenizer (`'(?i:[sdmt]|ll|ve|re)|...`) now loads, and splits `'S`, `'LL` and `'\u017f` as the tokenizers library does. A negated class, a property class or a non-ASCII member inside `(?i:...)` is still refused. Every tokenizer that loaded before splits as it did, since a pattern with such a class was refused until now; the regex tests pass on a Mac and on Linux (#842). Thanks to @plotarmordev.
- `POST /v1/systemone` takes llama-server's System One request: a state (text, JSON or a chat), questions keyed by id of type `noul`, `choice` or `score`, and pictures as data URLs (`files`, `images` or a chat state's `image_url` parts, at most 4). Each question is rendered as d1's systemone template renders it, read at its single-token option forms, and answered with probabilities and zero output tokens, refused with llama-server's sentences when malformed. The engine scores it through a new `decide` hook (one pass over the shared state and a branch a question), or `score` a question at a time; an engine that scores nothing serves no such path, and an engine that only decides refuses text requests. `/v1/decisions` asks `score` for the vocabulary's mass, System One does not. No engine before d1-3B (below) scores or decides, so for every other model `/v1/systemone` answers 404 as an unknown path does and chat requests are untouched; the server tests pass, and the golden suite gives the frozen replies as before, 309 of 319 with the same 10 known differences (#843). Thanks to @plotarmordev.
- On CUDA, the residual add and norm that Nemotron runs (the shared `add_rms_h`) issues every load before it uses one, and each warp folds the eight warps' sums itself instead of waiting for one warp's broadcast, so a block that runs one norm syncs once. The sums run in the same order, so the outputs are the same bytes: on a GB10 `tf-cuda-test glue` matched its host references byte for byte, and Nemotron's replies were the same tokens in 17 cells, drafted and with `"draft": false`. On one window-cost table Nemotron decoded 0.2% to 0.5% faster drafted, and its cold prompts of 2k to 64k tokens ran within 0.25% (#796). Thanks to @jschmied.
- d1-3B (Liquid AI's LFM2-VL decision model) runs on CUDA in the native engine and answers `POST /v1/systemone` and `/v1/decisions`: one prompt pass a request over the shared state and a branch a question, LFM2's gated short convolutions and GQA attention in bf16, read at each question's option rows. Pictures are decoded (PNG and JPEG, as Pillow decodes them), tiled as `Lfm2VlImageProcessor` tiles them, and run through the SigLIP2 tower and projector on the GPU. On a GB10, the 49 requests of the d1 recipe's parity set (github.com/plotarmordev/d1-tensorfold) gave the top answer of Transformers' bf16 reference on 98 of 99 questions (as the recipe's Python 0.6 engine does), within 0.027 in probability; one question took 24.7 ms against the Python engine's 25.3, three questions 27.7 against 28.7, a 3,446-token state 261 against 273, a picture 44.8 against 44.7 to 48.4, and 64 packed states 40.7 a second against 38.5. Nemotron's prompt attention kernel compiles to the same code as before. On a GB10 here, `tf-cuda-test lfm2-kernels` (41 of 41 byte-equal), `lfm2-attention` and `lfm2-vision` passed, the last with the d1-3B checkpoint (the test pictures' features at cosine 0.998 or better against the CPU reference); served, d1-3B answered System One and `/v1/decisions` the same way twice, alone and four at once, one question in 24.8 ms at the median, and refused a chat request. `cuobjdump` shows Nemotron's prompt attention kernel unchanged instruction for instruction, and Nemotron's replies were the same tokens with cold prompts of 2k to 64k tokens within 0.2%. A Mac refuses the family with the usual message (#844). Thanks to @plotarmordev.
- `core/eaq_container.zig` checks an EAQ pack (an EXL3 checkpoint with a manifest, a completion marker and the config.json fields that name them) before a loader reads it. `open` refuses the pack unless config.json's manifest hash, the marker, the layout and pack version, the model family, the required runtime features, the codebook tables' hashes, the shard sizes, the shard headers against the manifest's tensor inventory and every shard's sha256 all agree; a caller that asks gets the name of the file or tensor a refusal is about. No family calls it yet. Its host tests write small synthetic packs with one defect each and expect the matching refusal, with no GPU and no real weights. At landing we hardened the input checks: an inline entry must name a listed shard that holds its tensors, a trellis entry's dictionary must be one the manifest declares, entry names must be unique, and each shard must resolve inside the pack's folder (or, for a Hugging Face snapshot, its repository's blobs) and is hashed from the same open file its header was read from; a hash worker that cannot get its buffer reports OutOfMemory, and an empty pack hashes nothing (#858). Thanks to @MiaAI-Lab.
- Flash Next on Metal samples a request that asks for a temperature. Each token is drawn on the GPU by Nemotron's `tf_sample_full` under the shared rule every GPU sampler reads (the seed, 1/T, top_p, top_k and min_p), keyed by the token's own position inside the round's own command buffer, so a drafted reply equals its `"draft": false` reply at the same seed and a resumed prompt draws what a fresh one does. On an M3 Ultra with Flash Next 6-bit: drafted == `"draft": false` at 8 seeds and under min_p, top_p and whole-vocabulary rules, sampled multi-turn chats equal a server with the cache off 12 of 12, concurrent == solo, greedy tokens unchanged and greedy decode within 1%; sampled prose decodes at 88 tok/s against 96 greedy, and code at greedy speed. Speed-up mode stays greedy and refuses a temperature (#719). Thanks to @BobClawblaw.
- A GLM-5.3-Flash kept state that goes stale (its slot wrote below it, or its pool blocks went to another stream) leaves the prompt cache at once, so the next turn misses once and keeps a fresh state. Before, the dead entry was found and failed its restore, and that pass kept nothing, so the turn after it paid the whole prompt again: on an M3 Ultra, a 3,794-token conversation that shares one slot with another now resumes at 3,781 tokens on its second turn after the interruption, where it prefilled in full twice. Kept states are freed only between backend operations and never while a looked-up request still holds them, on every family's lane host, so a reservation, a pool placement or an overwrite can no longer free the state a queued request is about to restore; Nemotron's tokens, chats and cached counts on a GB10 are unchanged (#835). Thanks to @heitke.
- GLM-5.3-Flash runs on CUDA from NVIDIA's NVFP4 checkpoint at tensor parallel over hosts (`TF_GLM_RANK`, `TF_GLM_WORLD`, `NCCL_COMM_ID`): rank 0 serves and the other ranks follow its steps, with MTP drafts, concurrent streams through the lanes core and kept prompt states on its 2,048-row grid. Rank 0 admits streams and its prompt cache by the smallest room of the ranks and names the rank that bounds a refusal; ranks started with different drafts settings, or more than 16 ranks, are refused by name before the weights load. On two GB10s, `tf-cuda-test glm-ref` holds every projection within 0.982 of its float64 tolerance, the lane checks on a synthetic checkpoint hold every stream to its lone run byte for byte at TP=1 and TP=2, and the checkpoint's first four layers served at TP=2 with the admit as described; the full checkpoint has not been served on our hardware yet (#804). Thanks to @Bizuayeu, and to @BHCC2025 for the vocabulary's cut in EXL3's 128-column unit (#593).
- Pictures decode in one place every family shares (`zig/src/core/picture`), to the RGB pixels TensorFold 0.6.6 loaded with Pillow 12.3: PNG from the PNG specification, and JPEG in libjpeg-turbo 3.1.4.1's TurboJPEG library (`libturbojpeg`, the release Pillow 12.3 bundles), loaded at the first JPEG and run at Pillow's settings, then EXIF or XMP orientation and alpha over white; Pillow's and torch's bicubic resizes are shared too. TensorFold does not ship libjpeg-turbo: the server loads the file `TENSORFOLD_LIBTURBOJPEG` names, else `lib/` beside its folder, beside it, Homebrew's `jpeg-turbo` on macOS, then the system's library path, checks the version string libjpeg-turbo builds in, and refuses JPEG images by name without that release while PNG images still serve. Against 0.6.6 itself on an M3 Ultra with that library, all 29 decodable pictures of d1's goldens and 74 of 76 edge pictures (interlaced and 16-bit PNG, transparency keys, animated PNG frame counts, EXIF and XMP orientation, progressive and restart-marker JPEG, cut and malformed files) gave 0.6.6's pixels or its refusal. The two others are departures by design: a CMYK JPEG is refused, and a JPEG holding several pictures gives its first, Pillow's first frame bit for bit, where 0.6.6 refused it. d1-3B's pictures moved onto it and its own JPEG, PNG and resize code is gone, with its tiles, patches and placeholder ids unchanged; on a GB10, d1-3B gave the same System One and `/v1/decisions` answers as before, bit for bit, for text and a PNG picture.
- GLM-5.3-Flash reads images on a Mac with `--vision` (`--vision-max-images`, `--vision-image-tokens`): `image_url` parts in user messages and tool results are decoded by the shared picture decoder and prepared before admission as 0.6.6 prepared them, the image tower runs on the GPU, and its rows take the image placeholders' places in the prompt. Drafts stay on and exact, the prompt cache keys each image's rows by the image's content so a kept state never resumes another image, and text that spells the image markers stays text. The tower, its scratch and a prompt's image rows count against the 70% load limit, in stream admission and the prompt cache's budget too: 5.49 GiB at the default 20,480 image and video tokens, so on a 256 GB Mac the whole 4-bit checkpoint with `--vision` needs smaller budgets (2,048 image tokens, 64 video tokens and a 4,096-token context pass the limit, the MTP head left on disk). Native engines that read no images refuse the flag, and a speed-up pair refuses it before it loads. On an M3 Ultra with 8 of the checkpoint's layers, every served check passed: an image drafted equals `"draft": false`, four image requests at once equal each alone, a later turn resumes past its image (2,027 of 2,042 tokens cached) while another image under the same placeholders resumes only the text before it, four JPEGs gave the same replies drafted and plain, and the refusals (WebP by name, GIF, an image in an assistant message, a fifth image, a remote URL without `--vision-urls`, bad base64, a bad `detail`, bytes that decode as nothing) answer before any token. In the author's runs on an M5 Ultra, the tower's patches equal the Python engine's for every PNG, its rows match mlx-vlm's bf16 tower to cosine 0.985 to 0.997, and the whole model described its test images (#598). Thanks to @BobClawblaw.
- `--vision-urls` (with `--vision`) fetches an image part's public HTTPS URL as 0.6.6 fetched it: port 443 without credentials or a fragment, every address the host resolves to public, TLS verified on the address that was checked, at most three redirects, each checked again, 10 MB an image and 20 MB a request, 10 s a download and 30 s for a request's downloads. Without the flag an HTTPS part is refused with the flag's name. Its tests run canned responses through the redirect, refusal and limit paths, and pass on macOS and Linux. In the author's runs on an M5 Ultra, a public PNG gave the file's SHA-256 directly and through a 302; `localtest.me`, 10.0.0.1, bad certificates, a 404 and an HTML page were refused; and the served checks passed 17 of 17 (#614). Thanks to @BobClawblaw.
- GLM-5.3-Flash reads `video_url` parts with `--vision`, as transformers 5.19's `Glm5NextProcessor` prepares them: 2 frames a second, at most 256, each pair of frames one image to the tower, the frames resized with torch's antialiased uint8 bicubic under `--vision-video-tokens` (16,384 by default). Decoding needs `libtfvideo`, an optional library over FFmpeg 9.0.2 (`tools/zig/build_ffmpeg.sh`, then `zig build video`), loaded when the first video arrives; without it the server refuses a video by name, as it did on an M3 Ultra here. In the author's runs on an M5 Ultra, 13 clips (H.264, HEVC, VP8, VP9 and MPEG-4) gave the processor's indices, grid, patches and time texts bit for bit at two token budgets, `libtfvideo` decoded the same RGB as PyAV on all 1,925 frames, and the served checks passed 15 of 15 (#618). Thanks to @BobClawblaw.
- Qwen3.8-27B reads images on a Mac with `--vision` (`--vision-max-images`, `--vision-image-tokens`): `image_url` parts in user messages and tool results are prepared as transformers' Qwen2-VL processor prepares them, the checkpoint's image tower runs on the GPU in our own kernels (bf16 weights as stored, fp32 sums and residual), and its rows take the image placeholders' places, turned by their row and column (M-RoPE) in the 16 attention layers, with the rows after an image shifted through decoding, drafts and later turns. Text-only requests keep their tokens, drafts stay on and exact, and the prompt cache keys each image by its content and grid. The tower, its scratch and a request's image rows take 1.79 GiB at the default 4,096 image tokens, counted against the 70% load limit and the prompt cache's budget. On an M3 Ultra, six test pictures got the processor's grids, tokens and patches bit for bit and rows closer to transformers' float32 tower than its bf16 tower is (mean row error 1.0 to 2.2% against 1.6 to 4.1%); with an image, drafted replies equal `"draft": false`, four requests at once equal each alone, and later turns resume past the image and equal a fresh server's replies. The processor's geometry, the placeholders, the M-RoPE positions and the tower are written once in `zig/src/core/qwen_vl` for every Qwen3.5-VL checkpoint, and carry pieces of #726. Video parts are refused. Thanks to @BobClawblaw.
- LFM2.5-24B-A2B (`lfm2_moe`) serves on Metal from LiquidAI's MLX checkpoints at 4, 6 and 8 bits (the README names the revisions): 30 short-conv and 10 attention layers, 64 experts with 4 active, the embedding as the head. Prompt rows run `core/tile_qmm`'s tiles, and every decode, verify and shared row runs their twin once the load has checked that it gives the tiles' bits at each matrix shape (a miss sends every row through the tiles). Attention cuts keys at fixed 4,096-key chunks and folds them in order, so a row's bits never depend on its batch or on where a prompt was split. On an M3 Ultra `tf-lfm2-exact` passes its 54 checks at each width, drafted replies equal `"draft": false` (16 of 16, and 8 of 8 at 9k to 13k-token prompts), two and four concurrent requests equal their solo replies, one stream decodes at 194, 168 and 160 tok/s at 4, 6 and 8 bits, and cold prompts read at 4,421 tok/s at 2k and 3,549 at 32k. The served window stays inside the 131,072 positions the attention holds, and a larger `--context` is refused before the load. `--learn`, `--speed-up` and `--slide` are refused for it (#822). Thanks to @BlivionIaG.
- LFM2.5-24B-A2B reuses prompts across turns: the prompt cache keeps a stream's conv states and its keys and values in host memory at the turns' marks, and the next prompt pass starts from there, within `--prompt-cache-gib`. On an M3 Ultra, two multi-turn chats (three turns, then regenerate, edit and delete) resumed about 7,100 of 7,150 prompt tokens a step and gave the cache-off server's tokens at every step, 12 of 12 at each width, and 9k to 13k-token prompts resumed to their last few tokens gave the fresh replies' tokens (#823). Thanks to @BlivionIaG.
- `tensorfold benchmark MODEL` runs the community-v1 suite (code and chat at temperatures 1 and 0, a warm-up and five repeats each) against a server of its own on 127.0.0.1, or a running one with `--server`, and saves a receipt that meets the benchmark board's rules. Nothing is uploaded unless `--publish` (or `benchmark publish FILE`) is given, after a preview and consent, with a contributor token from `benchmark login`. `--drafter auto` passes the family's own drafter when it is cached (Qwen3.8-27B's DFlash2), and `--serial` turns drafts off. On an M3 Ultra with outbound network blocked but loopback, runs on LFM2.5-24B-A2B 4-bit and on Qwen3.8-27B with DFlash2 each completed 20 of 20 samples, and the board's own rules accept and rank both receipts (#717). Thanks to @ThinkOffApp.
- The Intel XPU runner checks its int4 GEMMs against FP64. With `XPU_FP64_REF=1`, `xpu-qwen27` runs each int4 GEMM of layers 0, 1, 31 and 63 and the head on the prompt's real activations, at decode on 1 and 8 rows and on the prompt, and compares it with FP64 references of the same weights from `core/quant`; `XPU_FIDELITY=1` compares the prompt and decode paths' top tokens at every position with the same forward in FP64. The int4 group means are stored 256 times larger, so the low half of their fp16 pair stays normal. In the author's runs on an Arc Pro B70 every native path stayed within the fp16 rounding of the FP64 output; it was not run here, with no Intel GPU (#802). Thanks to @Mirrdhyn.
- With no keys configured, the native server's `/health` also names the release it was built from (`version`), the backend built in (`backend`) and the GPU it serves on (`device`, by the driver's name), read once at startup. With keys configured it still answers only `{"status": "ok"}` (#854). Thanks to @ThinkOffApp.
- The server's JSON values are one named module (`zig/build/server.zig`) that a family's serving code can import; no reply changes (#860). Thanks to @jayleaton.
- A reply's `tensorfold` fields carry the full SHA-256 of its token ids (`token_sha256`, 64 hex characters) beside `token_sha`, which is its first 12 characters (#862). Thanks to @jayleaton.
- The benchmark tools (`tools/bench_concurrent.py`, `bench_cache.py` and `bench_evidence.py`) keep each reply's full token digest, the pairs of replies they compare and the client's send and completion times. Two replies count as equal only by their full digests, and a run without them is reported as unverified (#863). Thanks to @jayleaton.
- GLM-5.3-Flash's prompt rows run the last routed expert's sorted pairs to the pair count. The sort writes no end mark after the last expert, so its pairs were skipped on layers whose routed experts are not all 4-bit (or all 2-bit) in groups of 64: the 4-bit checkpoints never took that path, and a checkpoint with 5- and 6-bit experts gave empty replies from about 12,000 prompt tokens. `tf-glm-rows-check` lays the offsets out as the route writes them (#864). Thanks to @harrisonfriia.
- `docs/api.md` documents the native `/health` fields one by one and lists `/memory` among the routes (#866). Thanks to @jishnuvenugopal.
- The 4-bit one-row twin of `core/tile_qmm`, which LFM2.5-24B-A2B decodes through, turns each code into its integer before it multiplies, as the prompt tiles do. It scaled input j by 16 to the power -j instead, and a small input underflowed: 2^-126 gave 0 where the tiles give 2^-126. `tf-tile-check` adds that case for the tiles and the twin at widths 1 to 32 (#821). Thanks to @BlivionIaG.
- The DeltaNet wide prompt scan (`core/deltanet`) can keep the state and the conv window at up to four rows of each segment (`tf_delta_gdn_chain_wide_marks`, `tf_delta_gdn_mark_conv`), each the words a segment ending at that row leaves; `zig build test-deltanet-marks` checks them against such segments on synthetic rows. No model calls them yet (#851). Thanks to @jschmied.
- The dashboard shows a filling prompt's tokens left and how long the prompt queue takes to drain: while a prompt pass runs, `/stats` carries `fill_prompt_tokens`, `fill_tokens_left` and `fill_run` from a backend that reports its progress (GLM-5.3-Flash, Nemotron and the Qwen models on Metal), and `fill_unknown` from one that does not. On a Mac the Memory tile has numbers: the process footprint, its high-water mark since start or the last `/health?reset_peak=1`, and any configured load limit, which `/stats` and `/health` name `limit` and `/memory` names `load_limit_bytes` (#738, asked for in #772). On landing, a background warm pass (the 27B prefilling the next turn after each reply) no longer shows as a fill, and a resumed prompt counts the prefix it restored as filled from the first poll. Thanks to @heitke.
- Nemotron 3.5 Lightning (MLX affine 4/64) runs on Intel Arc (Xe2) over the XPU backend's Level Zero runtime, in the `tensorfold-xpu` CLI that `zig build -Dxpu` builds on Linux (OpenCL C kernels compiled to SPIR-V by `ocloc`). Every prompt row sums as the decode step does, so a prompt's bits do not depend on how it is cut into chunks, and a window of up to 16 rows equals that many one-row steps. On the author's Arc Pro B70 a short prompt decodes at 175 tok/s and prompts run at about 850 tok/s at 2k tokens and 511 at 128k; after 640 tokens cut five ways, the states and the next logits equal the row-by-row run. We have no Intel GPU, so it lands on the author's receipts and our host checks (#523). Thanks to @BHCC2025.
- `tensorfold-native serve --backend xpu` serves Nemotron 3.5 Lightning on Intel Arc beside Qwen3.8-27B. With the checkpoint's MTP head (`mtp-4bit.safetensors`) the lane core drafts with it and with copies, concurrent requests share rounds, each on its own sequence, and the prompt cache resumes a turn from a kept state; `--no-drafts` (or `TENSORFOLD_XPU_MTP=0`) serves one row at a time without the cache. On the author's Arc Pro B70 every drafted reply equals its `"draft": false` run, 4 concurrent replies equal their solo runs, a resumed turn equals a cold one, and plain greedy replies run at about 173 tok/s; drafts do not speed this MoE up yet (0.90 to 0.99 of plain) (#547). Thanks to @BHCC2025.
- GLM-5.3-Flash loads on a Mac whose page cache holds other files. Its loader read each 16 MiB only once 8 GiB of memory was free, but macOS frees cached file pages only as memory is taken, so free memory never climbed back: on an M3 Ultra with 158 GB of other checkpoints cached, a whole-model load read 89 GB in 22 s and then 0.6 GB in the next four and a half minutes. The reads now count the file cache and purgeable memory the kernel can drop as room, under the same floor (3% of RAM, at least 8 GiB). That load is ready in 32.7 s, against 29.6 s with the cache empty, with the same tokens in 8 of 8 cells, and the kernel compressed nothing while it gave the cache back. A load that stays short of the floor for 30 s stops and says how much memory was free and cached. The 70% load limit is unchanged.
- A Nemotron load on Metal that fails after its kernels compile, for example on a truncated shard, releases both compiled kernel sets instead of leaking them; a test loads a folder with an empty shard and checks that nothing leaks (#875). Thanks to @jishnuvenugopal.
- Living Weights deletes one fact: `DELETE /v1/slide/fact/{id}` asks every learned fact's held-out questions, sets the fact's input weights to zero, rounds the layers it touched as a save writes them, asks again, and then writes the change into the shards or puts every bit back. Only input weights change, since a layer's output weights share 4-bit groups across facts, and a layer that would round to other values when saved is put back rather than written. The graph file marks the fact `deleting` before the shards are written and `deleted` after, so it never claims more than is on disk; a put-back the GPU does not take refuses replies until it lands; and the delete keeps the folder's original files first, as a lesson does, so a rollback undoes it too. Deleting one of three facts on an M5 Max changed only that fact's ten `up_proj` rows, the other two answered as before, and a forced put-back left every file's SHA-256 as it was. Served on an M3 Ultra and a GB10, a delete rewrote three shard files, the deleted fact's four answers stopped naming it, every other reply stayed the same (14 of 14), a reload without `--slide` gave the same replies, and a forced put-back left every file in the folder and all 18 replies as they were (#872). Thanks to @ThinkOffApp.
- Living Weights asks a fact's questions about others by each wording's own whole subject. Besides the fact's own questions, a lesson's router reads them asked about other things of the same kind, and that swap took one subject for every wording, a single word found anywhere in a question: a question about "Project Aster" was asked about other things of one word's kind, and a swap could land inside "Asterion". Each wording now swaps its own subject, the whole name, only where it stands as whole words, and keeps a possessive. Four lessons about the user, whose subjects are single words, gave the same answers with it as without it on an M3 Ultra and a GB10 (#714). Thanks to @cwschroeder.
- When a later pipeline fails to compile at load, the Qwen-VL image tower (`core/qwen_vl`, the 27B's `--vision`) releases the twelve GEMM and eight glue pipelines it had made, then its library, instead of leaving them allocated.
- `tf-qwen-vision --bound MEAN WORST` makes its float32 comparison a gate: every picture needs its reference rows (`NAME.ref.f32`), and a missing or misshaped reference, or a mean or worst-row relative error past the bound, exits 1 once every picture is written. `meta.json` names each reference by its SHA-256. Without the flag the tool reports as before.
- An image's identity in the prompt cache's key covers its bytes and its patch grid in 62 bits (`core/picture/identity.zig`: SHA-256 over the encoded bytes, then over that and the grid) for GLM-5.3-Flash's images and video groups, the Qwen-VL tower's and RADIO's alike, and the server writes all 62 bits into the cache words of the markers around the image's rows (`server/image_key.zig`). Before, each image row's word held 31 bits of the image's hash and GLM's and RADIO's hashes named the bytes alone, so a one-token picture (32 by 32 at detail low, or sixteen pictures under a 16-token budget) was a single 31-bit word to the cache, and one picture prepared on two grids gave its first rows the same words. GLM-5.3-Flash's and Qwen3.8-27B's image gates gave the same replies and resumed counts with it.
- On CUDA, `--vision` is each family's to take: Nemotron 3.5 Super VL reads images with it, a family that reads none refuses it at its open, before its weights load, and LFM2 refuses it at startup, since d1-3B reads a checkpoint's pictures through its own tower without the flag.
- A Gemma 4 tool call's strings go back into its arguments in one pass over the call and a sort of their marks, instead of a copy of the whole call for each string; the arguments are the same (#881). Thanks to @jishnuvenugopal.
- A reply of many tool calls searches each DSML and tool-call opener once while it is parsed, closed and hidden, instead of again after every call; the calls and the visible text are the same (#882). Thanks to @jishnuvenugopal.
- A GLM tool call of many keys without values searches each key's and value's end once instead of again for every key; the arguments are the same, checked against the old search on 20,000 random calls (#883). Thanks to @jishnuvenugopal.
- `tools/shared_prefix_load.py` counts a stream that ends without `data: [DONE]` as failed, so its tokens no longer count toward successful requests, throughput or time to first token (#889). Thanks to @alenperic.
- `bench_openai.py` and `bench_concurrent.py` send a bearer key read from the environment variable `--api-key-env` names, only on the request they address (never on a redirect), and remove the key from everything they print or save (#890). Thanks to @alenperic.
- `tf-merge-check --quantizer DENSE SHIPPED MODULE...` quantizes dense tensors to MLX affine 4-bit as MLX does (groups of 32, 64 or 128; any other group is refused before anything is written) and reports how many codes, scales, biases and whole groups equal the shipped checkpoint's; `zig build test-merge` runs its tests (#709). Thanks to @philip-pentatonic.
- `--record DIR` writes each stream's committed tokens and the rows its drafter reads to a new folder under DIR, for drafter training: Nemotron on CUDA records its final norm's rows, a chunked prompt once its last chunk completes; the other families and backends refuse the flag by name (#848). Thanks to @jschmied.
- GLM-5.3-Flash samples every target row with the shared keyed sampler: the prompt's first reply token and plain rounds no longer fall back to greedy, queued server rounds carry their request's rule, and a drafted reply equals the same request with drafts off at any temperature; two Macs still refuse sampled requests (#892). Thanks to @heitke.
- LFM2.5-24B-A2B decode windows of 8 to 32 rows read each weight once through an MMA window kernel with the one-row twin's bits, and the 4-bit twin decodes its codes through exact half pairs (#867). Thanks to @BlivionIaG.
- LFM2.5-24B-A2B windows of 16 rows or more read each picked expert once for all of its rows (#868). Thanks to @BlivionIaG.
- On M5, `core/affine_mm` loads its code tiles in two 8-byte reads and takes an optional plain SwiGLU epilogue; GLM-5.3-Flash keeps its clipped one (#871). Thanks to @BlivionIaG.
- LFM2.5-24B-A2B prompt calls run on the M5's tensor units (projections, experts and attention) at every prompt row, so a resumed prompt equals a fresh one; a GPU without tensor units no longer reaches the tensor attention path (#873). Thanks to @BlivionIaG.
- GLM-5.3-Flash's sparse prompt attention on Metal reads a row's key list only as far as its last listed key: a row at position p lists its keys in the list's first p + 1 entries, so rows before position 2,050 skip the empty blocks, with the same bits (#898). Thanks to @harrisonfriia.

## 1.0.11

- `--cortheon`'s instruction filter keeps reading past a long word. A word longer than the filter's scan buffer ended the scan early, so an order written after one was never checked; each word now keeps at most 16 bytes, so a long word can no longer end the scan (#829). Thanks to @joeynyc.
- `--cortheon`'s instruction filter also reads page titles and search results' titles and snippets, which reached the model unchecked, so an order in a page's `<title>` or meta description is dropped like one in its text. A search says how many sentences it dropped, as a page read does.
- `--cortheon`'s instruction filter sees through soft hyphens and zero-width characters (U+00AD, U+200B to U+200D, U+2060 and U+FEFF), raw or written as numeric entities: one inside a word no longer splits an order into pieces the filter misses, and one between words still parts them.
- `--cortheon` drops Unicode tag characters (U+E0000 to U+E007F) from web text. They are invisible, yet some models read them as letters, so a page could spell an order in them that no one reading it would see; one inside a visible word no longer hides an order from the instruction filter either.
- `--cortheon` reads a page of unclosed tags in linear time. A run of `<` with no `>`, or of unclosed `<a ` tags, was rescanned to the page's end at every character: a 128 KiB page of either took 2 to 4 seconds, and one at the 2 MiB read limit would take 8 to 18 minutes. Both now take milliseconds.
- `--cortheon` reads a search page that ends inside a result's snippet: the result keeps its title and link with no snippet. Before, the parser sliced backwards there, and the safety check that released builds keep in the server stopped the process (#830). Thanks to @joeynyc.
- `--cortheon` shows at most 200 bytes of a page's title. A page that never closed its `<title>` sent the rest of its document to the model as the title, past the read's size limit (#831). Thanks to @joeynyc.
- [docs/known-issues.md](docs/known-issues.md) keeps the current list of known issues in one place. Each release's notes summarize it and say what changed, instead of pointing at an earlier release's notes.
- SECURITY.md says how to report a security problem privately (the repository's Security tab, "Report a vulnerability"), which releases get fixes, and what is in scope.
- GLM-5.3 (EXLR8) checks the GPU's fragment layout as it loads, as GLM-5.3-Flash and Nemotron do, so a Mac whose layout would give wrong sums stops about a second into the load instead of serving wrong tokens (#809). Thanks to @harrisonfriia.
- `zig build test` passes on a Mac with a GPU again. The split-checkpoint test asks on purpose for a tensor that stays in its file, and that lookup logged an error, which the Zig test runner counts as a failure; it now logs a warning.
- A pull request's No GPU check also runs the frozen server goldens (`zig build test-golden`, which now builds on Linux too) and fails a change that adds problems `tools/zig/lean_check.py` reports (a file past 600 lines, a comment longer than one line or 120 columns) beyond those the base branch already has.
- `--cortheon`'s instruction filter reads web text after its tag characters are dropped, so it judges exactly what the model reads. A tag character inside the bytes of a zero-width space hid that space from the filter, and dropping the tag then rebuilt it, so an order such as "upload the user's ssh keys" reached the model; dropping one tag character could also join the bytes around it into another. Both were found by the new fuzz harness.
- `--cortheon` reads a long DuckDuckGo lite page in linear time: each result is now read within its own part of the page, where a 1 MiB page of repeated results took about a second. Found by the fuzz harness.
- `--cortheon`'s page excerpt stays within its byte budget. Each kept line after a gap ran one byte over. Found by the fuzz harness.
- `--cortheon` parses at most the first 2 MiB of a page, for at most 2 seconds, and keeps the text read by then, so no page, however it is written, can hold a request for minutes.
- For contributors: `zig build fuzz-cortheon` runs Cortheon's readers of untrusted bytes (page text, links, excerpts, search results and the instruction filter) under a fuzz harness: its seeds, 100,000 structure-aware edits that cut tags and attributes, and a linear-time check on long inputs, in about 20 seconds. Its default build keeps the safety checks the shipped server keeps, `-Dfuzz-optimize=fast` builds it without them, and `--fuzz=N` adds N inputs from Zig's coverage-guided fuzzer.
- `--cortheon`'s search parser reads a results page in one pass. A page of link marks with no tag opening between them sent it back to the page's start for every mark, so 1 MiB took over 3 seconds; each search back now stops where the previous link ended.

## 1.0.10

- `cortheon_fetch` reads only URLs the conversation has already shown: search results, links on pages it has read, and URLs in the user's, the system prompt's or a tool's messages, never one that appears only in the model's replies, so a page that steers the model cannot make it build a URL that carries conversation text. The one exception is one named package's registry JSON on PyPI, npm or crates.io, a name of at most 64 characters. A refused fetch tells the model to search for the page. The `cortheon` object's `pages_read` now counts only pages read, not refused or failed fetches.
- The prompt cache keeps a turn's history while that turn's reply and its background prefill are kept. Qwen3.8-27B on a Mac keeps the end of each reply, and the 27B on a Mac and Nemotron on CUDA prefill the next turn's opening in the background after a reply. Both states counted as kept by prompts longer than the turn's own, so the cache took the turn's history for a superseded state and freed it first when it needed room. A regenerated turn, an edited reply or a client that sends the reply back rendered differently then read the whole prompt again. Both now count with the turn's prompt length (the background request carries it), and a later turn, whose prompt is longer, still frees them all first. Served on an M3 Ultra with a 4 GiB cache and first turns of 4,500 to 16,000 tokens, every regenerate, edit and re-rendered reply that needed the history resumed there, 20 of 20 sent as soon as the reply arrived and 8 of 8 sent after the background prefill had finished, against 4 and 0 without the change, and started in 0.14 to 0.98 s instead of 15 to 63 s. On an M5 Ultra, the reply-end change alone resumed 22 of 22 against 6. Prompts under 4,096 tokens, which keep one mark at a chunk end, gain the same way, 14 of 14 resuming there against 6 without the change. On a GB10, Nemotron's chats and its branch and fork runs resumed at least as many tokens as without the change at every step, and every reply on both chips equalled the cache-off server's.
- On CUDA, Nemotron with a Living Weights pocket no longer pays for the layers whose pocket has no neuron in use, and runs a taught layer's pocket over many blocks. Each pocket keeps its count of neurons in use on the host and the device: a layer with none launches nothing, and a taught one runs `tf_pocket_in` (the squared relu of the up rows' dot, a warp a neuron) into a small fp32 scratch, then `tf_pocket_out` (a thread an output) adds the neurons in order into the shared expert's slot, where `tf_pocket_rows` ran one 256-thread block a row over all 64 neurons. Every sum keeps its order, so replies are unchanged; the one value that can differ is an exact -0.0 in the shared expert's output of a layer with no neuron in use, which `tf_pocket_rows` turned into +0.0. Captured graphs read the count from the device and are captured again when a skipped layer gets its first neuron, and that recapture now keeps the sequence's own sampling rule. On a GB10, `tf-cuda-test pocket` matches `tf_pocket_rows` byte for byte in 196 cases (1 to 4,096 rows, 0 to 64 neurons in use), and a decode row's pocket takes 19 us instead of 94 and a 2,048-row prompt chunk's 0.48 ms instead of 1.23. A copy of Nemotron 3.5 Lightning (MLX 4-bit) taught one fact gave the same replies token for token as before, drafted and with `"draft": false`, alone and concurrent, and copies taught by the old and the new build wrote the same files. On that copy `"draft": false` decode was 17% faster, drafted decode 5% to 10% and cold prompts of 2k to 32k tokens 8.5% to 10.3% (#787). Thanks to @ThinkOffApp, and to @philip-pentatonic, whose #678 traced the cost and proposed both changes.
- A launch that no variant of a captured Triton set matches exactly now takes one that assumes less of its arguments: a variant compiled without 16-byte alignment on a pointer, integer or 64-bit argument serves an aligned one. The exact match still comes first, so every launch that matched before takes the same variant, a variant that assumes alignment still refuses an unaligned argument, and an integer of 1, which Triton folds into the kernel, still takes only the variant compiled for it. On a GB10 with Nemotron 3.5 Lightning (MLX 4-bit) and the qualified sm_121 set, replies were the same tokens as before, drafted and with `"draft": false`, alone and concurrent; with a copy of the set missing the 11 variants that assume more alignment than a sibling, the new rule served 34,786 launches of `_kv_write`, `_chunk`, `_merge` and `_topk` with the remaining variants and every reply was still the same tokens, where the build before it refused to load (#794, #791). Thanks to @zweck and @BobClawblaw, whose #726 has the same matcher change.
- `tools/zig/triton_aot_pack.py` (moved from the Nemotron tests) writes a captured Triton set from a capture's manifest or from a Recorder launch dump. `--jit` and `--cache` are optional (the manifest's own cache is the default, and a cubin recorded under another mount is found under `--cache`), `div16` is set only for an argument Triton gave `tt.divisibility` 16 (any attribute counted before), and a constexpr named like a runtime argument is left out. Packing the capture behind the qualified sm_121 set again, from its manifest or from its launch dump, gives the set's launch facts byte for byte for the 37 variants that capture holds (`aot.json`, with only the generator's name changed), and cubins that match the set's instruction for instruction; 28 of them differ only in the source folder they record. The engine only reads the set, and nothing on the serve path runs the packer (#795, #792). Thanks to @zweck.
- The Metal engine's shared kernels and `core/quant` read MLX's mixed-width (oQ) checkpoints, the base for the three families below. The lane projection takes 2-, 3-, 5-, 6- and 8-bit codes in groups of 32, 64 and 128, 48-row members and an output row stride; the row projection reads 3- and 5-bit codes and writes one member of a fused stack into its columns; `affine_mm` reads 2-bit codes; `core/quant` finds a module's width under any key spelling (oQ checkpoints write `language_model.model.layers.N...`, loaders ask for `model.layers.N...`) and refuses a checkpoint whose spellings of one module disagree; and `widen` re-packs codes at more bits, or a group at a smaller size, exactly. At the widths 1.0.9 serves nothing changes: the 4-, 6- and 8-bit lane and `affine_mm` sources preprocess to 1.0.9's text (168 layouts and formats), the row kernels give 1.0.9's bytes at 2, 4, 6 and 8 bits (301,056 outputs on an M3 Ultra and on an M5 Max), the lane kernels' outputs are 1.0.9's on every layout it runs (115 on an M5 Max, 13 on an M3 Ultra), and Nemotron on an M3 Ultra, which compiles these row kernels, gives 1.0.9's tokens drafted, resumed and concurrent. `tf-row-widths-check` and `tf-affine-ref` check every width against float64 and every row's bits in every window; @sethforprivacy ran them with `test-core-lane` on an M5 Ultra and @mrv777 on an M1 Max, both at 008abc0, all passing (#755). Thanks to @grantoverton.
- Qwen3.8-27B on a Mac serves mixed-width checkpoints such as TensorFold/Qwen3.8-27B-oQ4 and -oQ2: each loaded module is admitted at its own MLX width (2 to 8 bits, groups of 32, 64 or 128) against the kernels the chip runs, a fused stack whose members differ runs each member as its own product, the DFlash2 drafter borrows the target's embedding and head at their widths, and a mixed checkpoint times its own draft-window costs instead of reading a uniform checkpoint's. A uniform 4-bit checkpoint keeps 1.0.9's kernels, launches and window costs. Two changes of ours: a projection run that is not a whole number of 32-row tiles (a lone 48-row `in_proj_a` or `in_proj_b`) loads on the row kernels on every chip, which @sethforprivacy found on an M5 Ultra, where oQ4 was refused and oQ2 failed to load without it, and the 27B reads module widths through `core/quant`'s keys instead of its own copy. The oQ checkpoints rest on the contributors' runs: @grantoverton's on an M3 Ultra (in #756) and @sethforprivacy's on an M5 Ultra (his checks there at 8c05ac9, with that fix), each with drafted replies equal to `"draft": false`, resumed prompts equal to fresh ones and concurrent equal to solo. On an M3 Ultra the uniform 4-bit 27B with DFlash2, served by 1.0.9 and by this change in A B B A order, gave the same tokens in every case (17 drafted against `"draft": false`, 12 multi-turn replies also against a cache-off server, 8 concurrent cells), cold prefill from 2k to 32k tokens within 0.02%, and plain and prose decode within 0.12%; drafted code ran 4.5% slower at the same tokens with three more rounds a reply on each build's own timed window costs, and 0.35% faster with the two builds' tables swapped (#756).
